if the mechanical responses to the arbitrary deformation
\boldsymbol{\chi}
are identical, i.e.,
8
8

8

Our use of “proper” is not to indicate that any other theory is improper. It is simply to indicate that we are considering rigid motions
\boldsymbol{R}
that fix the normal vector and induce a rotation
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}
of the tangent plane
T_{\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}}\mathcal{S}^{*}=T_{\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}}\mathcal{S}
.

\displaystyle U(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{K}},\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}})=U^{*}(\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{K}}},\nabla^{*}\bar{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}}}).

(2.74)

By (
2.63
), (
2.66
), (
2.72
), (
2.73
), this requirement leads to the following definition: the rotation
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}
is in the
proper symmetry set
of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
relative to
\mathcal{S}
if for every smooth, invertible deformation
\boldsymbol{\chi}:\mathbb{E}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{E}^{3}
satisfying (
2.52
), we have

\displaystyle U(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{K}},\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}})=U\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}^{T},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{K}}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}^{T},\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}\bigl[\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathsf{E}}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}^{T}\bigr]^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{R}}^{T}\Bigr).

(2.75)

As in the case of the standard theory of Noll
[
35
]
, one can verify that the proper symmetry set of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
relative to
\mathcal{S}
is a subgroup of the group of rotations of
T_{\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}}\mathcal{S}
. In the case that the proper symmetry set of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
relative to
\mathcal{S}
equals the group of rotations of
T_{\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}}\mathcal{S}
, we say that the surface energy density
U
is
properly hemitropic
at
\boldsymbol{Y}_{0}
.

2.3.
Field equations

Following
[
48
]
, the field equations for the body
\mathcal{B}
with strain-gradient elastic surface
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\partial\mathcal{B}
are defined to be the Euler-Lagrange equations for the Lagrangian energy functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}[\boldsymbol{\chi}]=\Phi[\boldsymbol{\chi}]+V[\boldsymbol{\chi}]

(2.76)

where
V[\boldsymbol{\chi}]
is the potential energy associated to the applied forces.
9
9

9

See
[
45
]
for the case of a stand-alone elastic surface with no substrate.
In this work, we assume that the Gâteaux derivative of the load potential takes the form

\displaystyle\dot{V}=-\int_{\mathcal{B}}\boldsymbol{f}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,dV-\int_{\mathcal{S}}\boldsymbol{t}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,dA,

(2.77)

where
\boldsymbol{f}
is a prescribed external body force on
\mathcal{B}
and
\boldsymbol{t}
is a prescribed boundary traction on
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\chi}(\cdot;\epsilon)
be a one-parameter family of deformations of
\mathcal{B}
such that

\boldsymbol{\chi}(\cdot;\epsilon)|_{\partial\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{S}}=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\cdot)
, and denote

\displaystyle\dot{}:=\frac{d}{d\epsilon}\Big|_{\epsilon=0},\quad\boldsymbol{u}:=\dot{\boldsymbol{\chi}}(\cdot;\epsilon).

(2.78)

Then
\boldsymbol{u}|_{\partial\mathcal{B}}
vanishes to first order on
\partial\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{S}
.
Using the chain rule and integration by parts we have the classical identity

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}\dot{W}\,dV=\int_{\partial\mathcal{B}}\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,dA-\int_{\mathcal{B}}\mbox{Div}\,\boldsymbol{P}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}\,dV

(2.79)

where
\boldsymbol{P}=\mathchoice{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\boldsymbol{e}^{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{a}
is the Piola stress with
\mathchoice{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\frac{\partial W}{\partial\mathchoice{F^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.86128pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{F^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.86128pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{F^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.86128pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{F^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.87215pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.32147pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.66785pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[0.86128pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}}
and
\mbox{Div}\,\boldsymbol{P}=\bigl(\partial_{X^{a}}\mathchoice{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{P^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\big)\boldsymbol{e}^{i}
.
Using the chain rule we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}\dot{U}\,dA=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{T}}^{\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{,\alpha}+\boldsymbol{\mathsf{M}}^{\alpha\beta}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{,\alpha\beta}\Bigr)dA,

(2.80)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathsf{T}}^{\alpha}:=\frac{\partial U}{\partial y^{k}_{,\alpha}}\boldsymbol{e}^{k},\quad\boldsymbol{\mathsf{M}}^{\alpha\beta}:=\frac{\partial U}{\partial y^{k}_{,\alpha\beta}}\boldsymbol{e}^{k}.

(2.81)

We define surface stress vectors
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{P}}^{\alpha}
by

\displaystyle\quad\boldsymbol{\mathsf{P}}^{\alpha}:=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{T}}^{\alpha}-G^{-1/2}(G^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{M}}^{\alpha\beta})_{,\beta},

(2.82)

and use (
2.79
), (
2.80
) and integration by parts to obtain the Euler-Lagrange equations associated to the Lagrangian energy functional (
2.76
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mbox{Div}\,\boldsymbol{P}+\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mbox{on }\mathcal{B},\\
&\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{N}=G^{-1/2}(G^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{P}}^{\alpha})_{,\alpha}+\boldsymbol{t},\quad\mbox{on }\mathcal{S},\\
&\boldsymbol{\chi}(\boldsymbol{X})=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}(\boldsymbol{X}),\quad\mbox{on }\partial\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{S}.\end{split}

(2.83)

3.
Small strain models

Our principle motivation for modeling an elastic solid with strain-gradient elastic boundary surface is the study of brittle fracture. In this setting (to be discussed more in the following section), the surface
\mathcal{S}
possessing strain-gradient surface elasticity will be the crack front and strains will be linearized, motivating the introduction of a small-strain surface energy density. In this section, we present a model uniform, properly hemitropic, small-strain surface energy density that requires the same material constants (with the same physical interpretations) as found in the narrower Steigmann-Ogden theory. In contradistinction, the surface energy incorporates the surface’s resistance to geodesic distortion and satisfies the strong ellipticity condition. Moreover, the surface energy density may be viewed as a geometric generalization of that introduced and advocated for by Hilgers and Pipkin in
[
20
,
22
]
.

3.1.
Hilgers-Pipkin surface energy

In what follows indices are raised and lowered using the reference metric
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{G}}
, but we note that
\boldsymbol{y}^{,\alpha}
are the dual vector fields to
\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha}
and are not given by
\boldsymbol{y}_{,\beta}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}
. For the surface
\mathcal{S}
, we propose the uniform, properly hemitropic surface energy density

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{s}}{2}(\mathchoice{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}})^{2}+\mu_{s}\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}\mathsf{E}^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{\zeta}{2}\Bigl[(\mathchoice{\mathsf{K}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\mak (3.2)

Here
\lambda_{s}
,
\mu_{s}
,
\zeta
and
\eta
are positive numbers that can be interpreted as the surface Lamé constants and pure bending moduli.

In the case that
\mathcal{S}
is contained in a plane with flat coordinates
(\theta^{1},\theta^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathsf{g}_{\alpha\beta}-\delta_{\alpha\beta}),\quad\mathsf{L}_{\mu\alpha\beta}=\boldsymbol{y}_{,\mu}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle U=\frac{\lambda_{s}}{2}(\mathchoice{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}})^{2}+\mu_{s}\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}\mathsf{E}^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{\zeta}{2}\Bigl|\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2}\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha\alpha}\Bigr|^{2}+\eta\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{2}|\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha\beta}|^{2}.

(3.7)

[

, and therefore, we refer to (

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{s}}{2}(\mathchoice{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}})^{2}+\mu_{s}\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}\mathsf{E}^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{\zeta}{2}\mathchoice{\mathsf{K}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3. (3.8)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{s}}{2}(\mathchoice{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}})^{2}+\mu_{s}\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}\mathsf{E}^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{\zeta}{2}\Bigl|\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2}(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha\alpha})\Bigr|^{2}+\eta\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{2}(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha\beta})^{2}

(3.9)

on the basis of (
3.7
) being analytically simpler than (
3.9
). Indeed, with little effort one sees that for (
3.7
),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathsf{T}}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{s}\mathchoice{\mathsf{E}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\mathsf{E}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}\delta^{\alpha\beta}+2\mu_{s}\mathsf{E}^{\alpha\beta})\boldsymbol{y}_{,\beta},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathsf{M}}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\zeta\Bigl(\sum_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{y}_{,\gamma\gamma}\Bigr)\delta^{\alpha\beta}+2\eta\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha\beta}.

(3.11)

Moreover, it is simple to see that (
3.7
) satisfies the
strong ellipticity condition
,

\displaystyle\forall(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{(}0,0)\},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{0}\},\quad\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\beta}\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\Bigl(\boldsymbol{C}^{\alpha\beta\delta\gamma}\mathsf{a}_{\delta}\mathsf{a}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{b}\Bigr)>0,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}:=\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial y^{i}_{,\alpha\beta}\partial y^{j}_{,\delta\gamma}}\boldsymbol{e}^{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}^{j},

(3.13)

while (
3.9
) does not (see (
3.28
) and (
3.30
) below). Physically, this may be viewed as a consequence of the surface energy (
3.2
) incorporating the surface’s resistance to geodesic distortion via also including dependence on the tensor
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{L}}
.

In general, for (
3.2
) we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathsf{T}}^{\alpha}=\frac{\partial U}{\partial\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha}}=\frac{\partial U}{\partial\mathsf{E}_{\beta\gamma}}\frac{\partial\mathsf{E}_{\beta\gamma}}{\partial\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha}}+\frac{\partial U}{\partial\mathsf{K}_{\delta\nu}}\frac{\partial\mathsf{K}_{\delta\nu}}{\partial\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha}}+\frac{\partial U}{\partial\mathsf{L}_{\beta\gamma\delta}}\frac{\partial\mathsf{L}_{\beta\gamma\delta}}{\partial\boldsymbol{y}_{,\alpha}}.

(3.14)

extstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}{\sigma}}}}{\Gamma^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}{\sigma}}}}{\Gamma^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}{\sigma}}}}\Bigr]\boldsymbol{y}_{,\beta}.

(3.24)

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathsf{M}}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\zeta\mathchoice{\mathsf{K}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\mathsf{K}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\mathsf{K}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{\mathsf{K}^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}+2\eta\mathsf{K}^{\alpha\beta}\Bigr]\boldsymbol{n}

(3.25)

\displaystyle\quad+\Bigl[\zeta(\mathsf{g}^{-1})^{\mu\gamma}\mathchoice{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}{\nu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}+2\eta(\mathsf{g}^{-1})^{\mu\gamma}\mathchoice{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\mathsf{L}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\Bigr]\boldsymbol{y}_{,\gamma}.

(3.26)

To see that the strong ellipticity condition is satisfied for (
3.2
), we compute

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}=\Bigl(\zeta(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\mu\nu}+\eta[(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\mu}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\beta\nu}+(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\nu}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\beta\mu}]\Bigr)\boldsymbol{I},

(3.27)

and thus, for all

(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{(}0,0)\}
and
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{0}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\beta}\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\Bigl(\boldsymbol{C}^{\alpha\beta\delta\gamma}\mathsf{a}_{\delta}\mathsf{a}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{b}\Bigr)=(\zeta+2\eta)[(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\beta}]^{2}|\boldsymbol{b}|^{2}>0.

(3.28)

For the classical surface energy (
3.9
) of Steigmann-Ogden type, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}^{\alpha\beta\mu\nu}=\Bigl(\zeta(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\mu\nu}+\eta[(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\mu}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\beta\nu}+(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\nu}(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\beta\mu}]\Bigr)\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n},

(3.29)

and thus, for all

(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\beta}\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\Bigl(\boldsymbol{C}^{\alpha\beta\delta\gamma}\mathsf{a}_{\delta}\mathsf{a}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{b}\Bigr)=(\zeta+2\eta)[(\mathsf{G}^{-1})^{\alpha\beta}\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\beta}]^{2}(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{b})^{2}.

(3.30)

In particular, (
3.30
) shows that the surface energy (
3.9
) satisfies the associated Legendre-Hadamard condition (see
[
15
,
21
]
)

\displaystyle\forall(\mathsf{a}_{1},\mathsf{a}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{(}0,0)\},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{0}\},\quad\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\beta}\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\Bigl(\boldsymbol{C}^{\alpha\beta\delta\gamma}\mathsf{a}_{\delta}\mathsf{a}_{\gamma}\boldsymbol{b}\Bigr)\geq 0,

(3.31)

but not the strong ellipticity condition since the right side of (
3.30
) is 0 for
\boldsymbol{b}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
and orthogonal to
\boldsymbol{n}
.

3.2.
Parameter values

Viewing
\mathcal{S}
as the midsurface of a flat, thin, uniform, isoptropic strip with thickness
h
and Lamé parameters
\lambda_{l}
,
\mu_{l}
, the works
[
22
,
43
]
suggest the values

\displaystyle\lambda_{s}=\frac{2\lambda_{l}\mu_{l}}{\lambda_{l}+2\mu_{l}}h,\quad\mu_{s}=\mu_{l}h,\quad\zeta=\frac{2\lambda_{l}\mu_{l}}{\lambda_{l}+2\mu_{l}}\frac{h^{3}}{24},\quad\eta=\frac{h^{3}}{24}\mu_{l}.

(3.32)

For (
3.32
), the Hilgers-Pipkin surface energy (
3.2
) agrees with Koiter’s classical shell energy
[
24
]
for homogeneous plane strain and pure bending deformations of a plate.

We note that Koiter’s shell energy has been derived as the leading order model in small thickness from classical nonlinear elasticity (see
[
22
,
43
]
), while, to the author’s knowledge, (
3.2
) has not. Moreover, most work deriving surface energies as a small thickness approximation to a three-dimensional strain energy concerns materials having reflection symmetry with respect to a midsurface, and the resulting models do not contain strain-gradients. A notable exception is the work
[
42
]
that derives surface energies from parent strain energies exhibiting aribrary symmetry, and materials without reflection symmetry yield models that do include strain gradients. Deriving the specific Hilgers-Pipkin surface energy (
3.2
) or related strain-gradient surface energies from three-dimensional nonlinear elasticity or strain-gradient elasticity will be addressed in future work.

3.3.
Linearized equations

We now compute the linearization of (
4.4
) about the reference configuration. For the substrate, we adopt a classical quadratic, isotropic energy density

\displaystyle W=\frac{\lambda}{2}(\mathchoice{E^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{E^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{E^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{E^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}})^{2}+\mu E_{ij}E^{ij}.

(3.33)

Here indices are raised using the flat metric on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
\lambda
and
\mu
are the Lamé constants for the three-dimensional solid. For the surface
\mathcal{S}
, the surface energy density is given by (
3.2
).

Let
\boldsymbol{u}:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a displacement field such that
\boldsymbol{u}|_{\partial\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{S}}=\boldsymbol{0}
and

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathcal{B}}\Bigl[|\boldsymbol{u}(X)|+|\mbox{Grad}\,\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{X})|\Bigr]+\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{2}\sup_{\boldsymbol{Y}\in\mathcal{S}}|\boldsymbol{u}_{,\alpha\beta}(\boldsymbol{Y})|\leq\delta_{0}.

(3.34)

Assume that the body force
\boldsymbol{f}
, boundary traction
\boldsymbol{t}
, and Dirichlet condition
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}
satisfy

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{f}|=O(\delta_{0}),\quad|\boldsymbol{t}|=O(\delta_{0}),\quad|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}-\mbox{Id}|=O(\delta_{0}).

(3.35)

If

\boldsymbol{\chi}(\boldsymbol{X})=\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{X}),

then
E_{ij}=\varepsilon_{ij}+O(\delta_{0}^{2})
,
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}+O(\delta_{0}^{2})
, and
\mathsf{K}_{\alpha\beta}=\mathsf{k}_{\alpha\beta}+O(\delta_{0}^{2})
where

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{u}}{\partial X^{j}}+\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\cdot\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{u}}{\partial X^{i}}\Bigr)=O(\delta_{0}),

(3.36)

and on
\mathcal{S}
,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\boldsymbol{Y}_{,\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{,\beta}+\boldsymbol{Y}_{,\alpha}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{,\beta}\bigr)=O(\delta_{0}),\quad\mathsf{k}_{\alpha\beta}=\boldsymbol{N}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{;\alpha\beta}=O(\delta_{0}),

(3.37)

see (3.12) in
[
48
]
. Now we observe that

\mathsf{L}_{\alpha\beta\delta}=\mathsf{l}_{\alpha\beta\delta}+O(\delta_{0}^{2}),
with

\displaystyle\mathsf{l}_{\alpha\beta\delta}

(3.43)

The linearization of (
2.83
) about the reference configuration is obtained by omitting the

terms from
\boldsymbol{P}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{T}}^{\alpha}
and

, yielding

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mbox{Div}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mbox{on }\mathcal{B},\\
&\boldsymbol{\sigma}\boldsymbol{N}=G^{-1/2}(G^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\mathsf{p}}^{\alpha})_{,\alpha}+\boldsymbol{t},\quad\mbox{on }\mathcal{S},\\
&\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mbox{on }\partial\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{S},\end{split}

(3.44)

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=\lambda(\textrm{tr}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon})\boldsymbol{I}+2\mu\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
and

over the set of

.

In what follows,
\mathchoice{\boldsymbol{g}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{g}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{g}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{g}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\delta^{\beta\mu}\boldsymbol{g}_{,\alpha\mu}
and
\boldsymbol{g}^{,\alpha\beta}=\delta^{\alpha\lambda}\delta^{\beta\mu}\boldsymbol{g}_{,\lambda\mu}
. For the case of the classical Hilgers-Pipkin surface energy (
3.7
), we see from (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathsf{t}}^{\alpha}=(\lambda_{s}\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}{\epsilon^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}}\delta^{\alpha\beta}+2\mu_{s}\epsilon^{\alpha\beta})\boldsymbol{Y}_{,\beta},\quad\boldsymbol{\mathsf{m}}^{\alpha\beta}=\zeta\delta^{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\boldsymbol{u}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89798pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.78427pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{,\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89798pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.78427pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{,\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89798pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.78427pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{,\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.83015pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89798pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.78427pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\gamma}}}_{{{,\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+2\eta\boldsymbol{u}^{,\alpha\beta}

(3.47)

Writing
\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}+\mathsf{u}^{3}\boldsymbol{N}=\mathsf{u}^{\gamma}\boldsymbol{Y}_{\gamma}+\mathsf{u}^{3}\boldsymbol{N}
, it follows that (
3.44
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mbox{Div}\,\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mbox{on }\mathcal{B},\\
&\boldsymbol{\sigma}\boldsymbol{N}=\mu_{s}\mathchoice{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{u}}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.8284pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49565pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.21117pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}_{{{,\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+(\lambda_{s}+\mu_{s})\mathchoice{\mathsf{u}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.03252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.02322pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\alpha\gamma}}}}{\mathsf{u}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.03252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.02322pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\alpha\gamma}}}}{\mathsf{u}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.03252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.02322pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\alpha\gamma}}}}{\mathsf{u}^{{{\gamma}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.02892pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.03252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.02322pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{,\alpha\gamma}}}}\boldsymbol{Y}^{,\alpha}-(\zeta+2\eta)\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}\mathchoice{\boldsymbol{u}^{{{,\alpha\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{{{,\alpha\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{{{,\alpha\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\boldsymbol{u}^{{{,\alpha\beta}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.36281pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.26721pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.19086pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+\boldsymbol{t},\quad\mbox{on }\mathcal{S},\\
&\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0},\quad\mbox{on }\partial\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{S}.\end{split}

(3.48)

We remark that if
\mathcal{B}
is bounded with a sufficiently smooth boundary,
\mathcal{S}\neq\partial\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{S}
has sufficiently smooth boundary,
\boldsymbol{f}\in L^{6/5}(\mathcal{B})
, and
\boldsymbol{t}\in L^{p}(\mathcal{S})
with
p>1
, then (
3.48
) has a unique weak solution in an appropriately defined energy space (see Theorem 2 in
[
8
]
).

4.
Mode-III Fracture Problem

In this section, we apply the linearized theory (
3.48
) to the problem of a brittle infinite plate, with a straight crack
\mathcal{C}
of length
2\ell
, under far-field anti-plane shear loading
\sigma
. As discussed in Section 1 and in contrast to ascribing either a quadratic Gurtin-Murdoch or Steigmann-Ogden surface energy (
3.9
) to the crack fronts, the use of (
3.2
) yields a model that predicts bounded strains and stresses up to the crack tips (see Theorem
4.4
).

4.1.
Formulation and governing equations

We consider a brittle, infinite plate under anti-plane shear loading,
\lim_{x^{2}\rightarrow\pm\infty}\sigma_{13}=0
and
\lim_{x^{2}\rightarrow\pm\infty}\sigma_{23}=\sigma
, with a straight crack
\mathcal{C}=\{(x^{1},0,x^{3})\mid x^{1}\in[-\ell,\ell]\}
of length
2\ell
(see Figure
2
). For anti-plane shear, the displacement field takes the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})=u(x^{1},x^{2})\boldsymbol{e}_{3},

(4.1)

Then the only nonzero components of the stress are

\displaystyle\sigma_{13}=\mu u_{,1},\quad\sigma_{23}=\mu u_{,2}.

(4.2)

By the symmetry of the problem,
u
can be taken to be even in
x^{1}
and odd in
x^{2}
, so we will focus only on the strain and stress fields for
x^{2}\geq 0
. The governing field equations are (
3.48
) on
\mathcal{B}=\{(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})\mid x^{2}\geq 0\}
with
\mathcal{S}=\{(x^{1},0,x^{3})\mid x^{1}\in[-\ell,\ell]\}
,
\boldsymbol{t}=\boldsymbol{0}
and
\boldsymbol{f}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Figure 2.
Schematic of the mode-III problem with the crack
\mathcal{C}
appearing in blue.

We define dimensionless variables

\displaystyle x=\frac{x^{1}}{\ell},\quad y=\frac{x^{2}}{\ell},\quad z=\frac{x^{3}}{\ell},\quad w(x,y,z)=\frac{1}{\ell}\Bigl(u(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})-\frac{\sigma}{\mu}x^{2}\Bigr).

(4.3)

Then the field equations take the dimensionless form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Delta w(x,y)=0,\quad y>0,\\
&-w_{y}(x,0)=\alpha w_{xx}(x,0)-\beta w_{xxxx}(x,0)+\gamma,\quad x\in(-1,1),\\
&w(x,0)=0,\quad|x|\geq 1,\\
&w_{x}(\pm 1,0)=0,\end{split}

(4.4)

with the decay condition
\lim_{y\rightarrow\infty}|\nabla w(x,y)|=0
. We note that the boundary conditions
w_{x}(\pm 1,0)=0
imply that the crack opening is cusp shaped rather then blunted (see also Figure
3
and Figure
4
). The dimensionless parameters
\alpha,\beta
and
\gamma
are given by

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{\mu_{s}}{\mu\ell}>0,\quad\beta=\frac{\zeta+2\eta}{\mu\ell^{3}}>0,\quad\gamma=\frac{\sigma}{\mu},

(4.5)

and in particular, we see from (
4.5
) that the behavior of the displacement
w
depends on the length of the crack,
\ell
. For macro cracks satisfying
\beta\ll\alpha\ll 1
, we expect
w(x,0)
to be well-approximated by the singular, rounded opening profile from the classical linear elastic fracture mechanics except in small regions near the crack tips (boundary layers). See Figure
4
.

We remark that in using the Steigmann-Ogden surface energy (
3.9
) rather than (
3.2
), the boundary conditions at
y=0
are replaced by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-w_{y}(x,0)=\alpha w_{xx}(x,0)+\gamma,\quad x\in(-1,1),\\
&w(x,0)=0,\quad|x|\geq 1.\end{split}

(4.6)

One may view this loss of higher order derivatives in the boundary conditions as a consequence of the fact that the Steigmann-Ogden surface energy does not satisfy the strong-ellipticity condition (
3.12
): for anti-plane shear,
\boldsymbol{b}=\boldsymbol{u}(x^{1},0,x^{3})
is orthogonal to the surface’s normal
\boldsymbol{n}=-\boldsymbol{e}_{2}
(see (
3.30
)). As discussed in
[
51
,
23
]
, the boundary conditions (
4.6
)
do not
lead to a model predicting bounded strains up to the crack tips
x=\pm 1
, i.e., the displacement field satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{y>0}|\nabla w(\pm 1,y)|=\infty.

(4.7)

We see that (
4.4
) is the system of Euler-Lagrange equations for the energy functional

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{L}[w]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\nabla w(x,y)|^{2}dxdy+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{2}|w_{x}(x,0)|^{2}+\frac{\beta}{2}|w_{xx}(x,0)|^{2}\Bigr)dx

(4.8)

\displaystyle\quad-\gamma\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}w(x,0)dx

(4.9)

defined for
w
with
{\nabla}w\in L^{2}(\{y>0\})
,
w(\cdot,0)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
w(x,0)=0
for all
|x|\geq 1
. Motivated by this observation, we define the Hilbert space
H
to be the completion of
C^{\infty}_{c}((-1,1))
under the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{H}:=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\bigl(\alpha|f^{\prime}(x)|^{2}+\beta|f^{\prime\prime}(x)|^{2}\bigr)dx.

(4.10)

It is straightforward to verify the following facts using the fundamental theorem of calculus and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

•

(Sobolev embedding) If
f\in H
then
f\in C_{c}^{1,1/2-}(\mathbb{R})
and
f(x)=0
for all
|x|\geq 1
, and for all
\delta\in[0,1/2)
, there exists a constant
A>0
depending on
\alpha,\beta
and
\delta
, such that for all
f\in H
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{1,\gamma}(\mathbb{R})}\leq A\|f\|_{H}.

(4.11)

•

f\in H
if and only if
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
f(x)=0
for all
|x|\geq 1
. Moreover, there exist
b,B>0
depending on
\alpha
and
\beta
such that for all
f\in H
,

\displaystyle b\|f\|_{H}\leq\|f\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq B\|f\|_{H}.

(4.12)

The problem (
4.4
) can be reduced completely to a problem on the boundary by using the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map
-w_{y}(x,0)=\mathcal{H}w_{x}(x,0)
where
\mathcal{H}
is the Hilbert transform

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}f(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\,\mbox{p.v.}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{f(s)}{x-s}ds,\quad f\in H.

(4.13)

Then finding
w
with
\nabla w\in L^{2}(\{y>0\})
and
w(\cdot,0)\in H
satisfying (
4.4
) is equivalent to determining
w(\cdot,0)=:f\in H

satisfying
10
10

10

Once
f
is found,
w
is determined on the upper half plane using the standard Poisson kernel for the upper half plane.

\displaystyle\beta f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(x)-\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x)=\gamma,\quad x\in(-1,1).

(4.14)

By using the Plancherel theorem, the Fourier representation of the Hilbert transform (see (
4.44
)), and (
4.12
), we have for all
f\in H
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\|f\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq B\|f\|_{H}.

(4.15)

Definition 4.1
.

A function
f\in H
is a
weak solution
to the integro-differential equation (
4.14
) if for all
g\in H

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}[\beta f^{\prime\prime}(x)g^{\prime\prime}(x)+\alpha f^{\prime}(x)g^{\prime}(x)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x)g(x)\Bigr]dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma g(x)dx.

(4.16)

We remark that since
f,g\in H
, the integrals appearing in (
4.16
) are in fact over the interval
(-1,1)
.

A function
f\in H
is a
classical solution
to (
4.14
) if
f\in C^{4}((-1,1))\cap H
and
f
satisfies (
4.14
) pointwise.

We note that by (
4.15
) and Cauchy-Schwarz, (
4.16
) is well-defined for each
f,g\in H
.

4.2.
Solution of the integro-differential equation

We now establish that there exists a unique classical solution to (
4.14
), and the solution’s behavior is consistent with the linearization assumption (
3.34
).
We denote the following Green function

\displaystyle G(x,\tau)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{24}(x-1)^{2}(\tau+1)^{2}(1+2x-2\tau-x\tau)&\quad\tau\in[-1,x],\\
\frac{1}{24}(\tau-1)^{2}(x+1)^{2}(1+2\tau-2x-x\tau)&\quad\tau\in[x,1],\end{cases}

(4.17)

satisfying
G_{xxxx}(x,\tau)=\delta(x-\tau)
,
G(\pm 1,\tau)=0
,
G_{x}(\pm 1,\tau)=0
. We note that
G(x,\tau)=G(\tau,x)
for all
\tau,x\in[-1,1]
,
G\in C^{2}([-1,1]\times[-1,1])
and
\int_{-1}^{1}G(x,\tau)d\tau=\frac{1}{24}(1-x^{2})^{2}
. In particular, we have for all
f\in H
,

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\tau\tau}(x,\tau)f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)d\tau=f(x).

(4.18)

Figure 3.
Numerical solutions for the equivalent formulation of (
4.14
) as a Fredholm problem (
4.36
). The parameters
(\beta,\alpha,\gamma)
range over
(1,1,1)
,
(5,1,5)
and
(10,1,10)
. For
\gamma=\beta
and
\beta\gg 1\simeq\alpha
, the opening profile is well approximated by the limiting opening profile
f_{\infty}(x)=\frac{1}{24}(1-x^{2})^{2}
on
[-1,1]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

A function
f\in H
is a weak solution to (
4.14
) if and only if
f
satisfies

\displaystyle\beta f(x)+\int_{-1}^{1}G(x,\tau)(-\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau=\frac{\gamma}{24}(1-x^{2})^{2},\quad x\in[-1,1].

(4.19)

Proof.

Let
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
h(x)=0
for all
|x|>1
, and set

\displaystyle g(x)=\begin{cases}\int_{-1}^{1}G(x,\tau)h(\tau)d\tau&\mbox{ if }|x|\leq 1,\\
0&\mbox{ if }|x|>1.\end{cases}

(4.20)

Since
G\in C^{2}([-1,1]\times[-,1,1])
,
G(\pm 1,\tau)=0
, and
G_{x}(\pm 1,\tau)=0
,
g
is twice continuously differentiable on
\mathbb{R}\backslash\{\pm 1\}
and continuously differentiable on
\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\{|x|\leq 1\}}(x)\int_{-1}^{1}G_{x}(x,\tau)h(\tau)d\tau,

(4.21)

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\{|x|\leq 1\}}(x)\int_{-1}^{1}G_{xx}(x,\tau)h(\tau)d\tau,

(4.22)

where
\chi_{E}
is the indicator function of a subset
E\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.
In particular, we conclude that
g\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and thus,
g\in H
. Inserting
g
into (
4.16
), integrating by parts in the second term and using that
g(\pm 1)=0
yield

\displaystyle\beta\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{xx}(x,\tau)f^{\prime\prime}(x)h(\tau)d\tau dx

(4.23)

\displaystyle+\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}G(x,\tau)(-\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x))h(\tau)d\tau dx

(4.24)

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}G(x,\tau)h(\tau)d\tau dx.

(4.25)

Interchanging the order of integration, using the symmetry of
G
and relabeling the integration variables lead to

\displaystyle\beta\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{\tau\tau}(x,\tau)f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)d\tau\,h(x)dx

(4.26)

\displaystyle+\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}G(x,\tau)(-\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau\,h(x)dx

(4.27)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\gamma}{24}(1-x^{2})^{2}h(x)dx.

(4.28)

Finally, by (
4.18
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\Bigl[\beta f(x)+\int_{-1}^{1}G(x,\tau)(-\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau\Bigr]\,h(x)dx

(4.29)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\gamma}{24}(1-x^{2})^{2}h(x)dx,

(4.30)

for all
h(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
h(x)=0
for all
|x|>1
, proving (
4.19
).

Conversely, if
f\in H
and (
4.19
) holds, then for all
g\in H
, we have

\displaystyle\beta\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f^{\prime\prime}(x)g^{\prime\prime}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}G_{xx}(x,\tau)(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))g^{\prime\prime}(x)dx

(4.31)

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\gamma}{6}(3x^{2}-1)g^{\prime\prime}(x)dx.

(4.32)

We again interchange the order of integration and use integration by parts and
\int_{-1}^{1}G_{xx}(x,\tau)g^{\prime\prime}(x)dx=g(\tau)
to conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}

\displaystyle G_{xx}(x,\tau)(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))g^{\prime\prime}(x)dx+\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{\gamma}{6}(3x^{2}-1)g^{\prime\prime}(x)dx

(4.33)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))g(\tau)d\tau+\int_{-1}^{1}\gamma g(x)dx

(4.34)

\displaystyle=-\int_{-1}^{1}(\alpha f^{\prime}(x)g^{\prime}(x)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x)g(x))dx+\int_{-1}^{1}\gamma g(x)dx

(4.35)

This proves
f\in H
satisfies (
4.16
) and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Figure 4.
Numerical solutions for the macro-crack regime
\beta\ll\alpha\ll 1
. The parameters
(\beta,\alpha,\gamma)
range over
(10^{-1},10^{-1},1)
,
(10^{-2},10^{-1},1)
,
(10^{-5},10^{-2},1)
and
(10^{-6},10^{-3},1)
. For
\beta\ll\alpha\ll 1
, we expect the crack opening
f(x)
to be well-approximated by the singular, rounded opening profile predicted by classical linear elastic fracture mechanics away from the crack tips where
f^{\prime}(\pm 1)=0
(and the profile is cusped).

Via integration by parts and straightforward computations, we conclude from Lemma
4.2
that the crack opening profile
f
must satisfy the following Fredholm integral equation of the second kind (
4.36
). We will show in Theorem
4.4
that this equation is uniquely solvable for
arbitrary

\alpha,\beta>0
and
\gamma
. We remark that since the kernel extends to a continuous function on
[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
(the singularities are removable), the numerical computation of solutions is relatively straightforward via the Nÿstrom method with the trapezoidal rule to approximate the integral (see Figure
3
and Figure
4
).

Corollary 4.3
.

A function
f\in H
is a weak solution to (
4.14
) if and only if
f
satisfies the Fredholm equation

\displaystyle\beta f(x)+\int_{-1}^{1}K(x,s)f(s)ds=\frac{\gamma}{24}(1-x^{2})^{2},\quad x\in[-1,1],

(4.36)

where
K(x,s)=-\alpha G_{ss}(x,s)+\frac{1}{\pi}\mbox{p.v.}\,\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{G_{\tau}(x,\tau)}{s-\tau}d\tau
,

\displaystyle G_{ss}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{4}(x-1)^{2}(2s+xs+1)&\quad s\in[-1,x],\\
-\frac{1}{4}(x+1)^{2}(-2s+xs+1)&\quad s\in[x,1],\end{cases}

(4.37)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\mbox{p.v.}\,\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{G_{\tau}(x,\tau)}{s-\tau}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}(sx-1)(x^{2}-1)+\frac{1}{2\pi}(s-x)^{2}\log|x-s|

(4.38)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{8\pi}(x-1)^{2}(-x+2s+sx)(1+s)\log(1+s)

(4.39)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{8\pi}(x+1)^{2}(x-2s+sx)(1-s)\log(1-s).

(4.40)

Theorem 4.4
.

There exists
C>0
depending on
\alpha
and
\beta
such that the following hold. There exists a unique classical solution
f
to (
4.14
), and
f
satisfies

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{4}([-1,1])}\leq C|\gamma|.

(4.41)

Moreover, the displacement field
w(x,y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}P_{y}(x-s)f(s)ds
, where
P_{y}(\cdot)
is the Poisson kernel for the upper half plane, has bounded stresses and strains up to the crack tips:

\displaystyle{\mu^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{\sigma}\|_{C(\{y\geq 0\})}}+\|w\|_{C^{1}(\{y\geq 0\})}\leq C|\gamma|.

(4.42)

Proof.

In what follows,
C
will denote a positive constant depending only on
\alpha
and
\beta
that may change from line to line, and we denote the Fourier transform and inverse Fourier transform by

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\xi)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)e^{-2\pi ix\xi}dx,\quad\check{f}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(\xi)e^{2\pi ix\xi}\,d\xi.

(4.43)

We recall that

\displaystyle\widehat{f^{\prime}}(\xi)=2\pi i\xi\hat{f}(\xi),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{H}f}(\xi)=-i{\mathrm{sgn}}(\xi)\hat{f}(\xi),

(4.44)

the latter relation following from the Fourier representation of
w
on the upper half plane (see (
4.50
)).

We define a bilinear form
B(\cdot,\cdot):H\times H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle B(f,g)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}[\beta f^{\prime\prime}(x)g^{\prime\prime}(x)+\alpha f^{\prime}(x)g^{\prime}(x)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x)g(x)\Bigr]dx,\quad f,g\in H.

(4.45)

By Cauchy-Schwarz, (
4.15
), and (
4.12
) we conclude that for all
f,g\in H
,

\displaystyle|B(f,g)|\leq(1+B^{2})\|f\|_{H}\|g\|_{H},

(4.46)

so that
B(\cdot,\cdot)
is a bounded bilinear form on
H
. Moreover, by the Plancherel theorem and (
4.44
), we have for all
f\in H
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x)f(x)dx=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}2\pi|\xi||\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\geq 0.

(4.47)

Thus, the bilinear form is coercive. Since for all
g\in H
,

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma g(x)dx\Bigr|\leq|\gamma|\|g\|_{C([-1,1])}\leq|\gamma|A\|g\|_{H},

(4.48)

the classical Lax-Milgram theorem implies that there exists a unique weak solution
f\in H
to (
4.14
), and moreover,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H}\leq A|\gamma|.

(4.49)

To prove (
4.42
), we express
w
via the Fourier transform,

\displaystyle w(x,y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-2\pi y|\xi|}e^{2\pi ix\xi}\hat{f}(\xi)d\xi.

(4.50)

Since
f\in H\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

\displaystyle\int(1+|\xi|)^{4}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\leq C_{0}\|f\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq C\|f\|_{H}^{2}.

(4.51)

Thus, by Cauchy-Schwarz

\displaystyle|w(x,y)

\displaystyle|+|\nabla w(x,y)|

(4.52)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1+2\pi|\xi|\sqrt{2})|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\xi

(4.53)

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\sqrt{2}\Bigl(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\xi|^{2}(1+|\xi|)^{-4}d\xi\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1+|\xi|)^{4}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}d\xi\Bigr)^{1/2}

(4.54)

\displaystyle\leq C\|f\|_{H}\leq C|\gamma|.

(4.55)

The bound on
\mu^{-1}\boldsymbol{\sigma}
then follows immediately from (
4.55
), (
4.3
) and (
4.2
).

We now show that the weak solution
f
is a classical solution, and (
4.41
) holds. By a density argument in
H
, we have that
\int_{-1}^{1}G(\cdot,\tau)(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau\in H^{3}([-1,1])
with

\displaystyle\frac{d^{k}}{dx^{k}}\int_{-1}^{1}

\displaystyle G(x,\tau)(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau

(4.56)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\partial_{x}^{k}G(x,\tau)(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau,\quad k=1,2,

(4.57)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{3}}{dx^{3}}\int_{-1}^{1}

\displaystyle G(x,\tau)(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau

(4.58)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{x}\frac{1}{4}(2-\tau)(\tau+1)^{2}(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau

(4.59)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{x}^{1}\frac{1}{4}(2+\tau)(\tau-1)^{2}(\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(\tau)-\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(\tau))d\tau.

(4.60)

By Lemma
4.2
, we conclude that
f\in H^{3}([-1,1])
, and by (
4.19
) (
4.60
), Cauchy-Schwarz, and (
4.49
) we have

\displaystyle\|f^{\prime\prime\prime}\|_{L^{2}([-1,1])}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+|\gamma|)

(4.61)

\displaystyle\leq C(\|f\|_{H}+|\gamma|)\leq C|\gamma|.

(4.62)

Moreover, by (
4.49
), (
4.62
) and the fundamental theorem of calculus,
f\in C^{2}([-1,1])
with

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{2}([-1,1])}\leq C|\gamma|.

(4.63)

By (
4.16
) and integration by parts, it follows that for all
g\in C^{\infty}_{c}((-1,1))\subset H
,

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}f^{\prime\prime\prime}(x)g^{\prime}(x)dx=\frac{1}{\beta}\int_{-1}^{1}[-\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x)-\gamma]g(x)dx,

(4.64)

and, thus,
f\in H^{4}([-1,1])
and

\displaystyle\beta f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}(x)-\alpha f^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}(x)=\gamma,\quad\mbox{for a.e. }x\in(-1,1).

(4.65)

Then by (
4.65
), (
4.63
), and the fact that
\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow C(\mathbb{R})
, we conclude that
f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\in C([-1,1])
and

\displaystyle\|f^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}\|_{C([-1,1])}

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\|f\|_{C^{2}([-1,1])}+\|\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}\|_{C([-1,1])}+|\gamma|\Bigr)

(4.66)

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\|\mathcal{H}f^{\prime}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R})}+|\gamma|\Bigr)

(4.67)

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\|f\|_{H}+|\gamma|\Bigr)\leq C|\gamma|.

(4.68)

By (
4.62
), (
4.63
) and (
4.68
), it follows that
f\in C^{4}([-1,1])
is a classical solution to (
4.14
) and (
4.41
) holds.
∎

C. Rodriguez

Chapel Hill, NC 27599, USA
